Stochastic Kimura Equations Thanks: Part of this work is supported by the NSERC Discovery Grant of the second author (No. 241023). Thanks: † \dagger roland.riachi@mail.mcgill.ca Thanks: ‡ \ddagger linan.chen@mcgill.ca
Roland Riachi †, Linan Chen ‡

Abstract
. In this work we study the one-dimensional stochastic Kimura equation ∂ t u ⁡ ( z , t ) = z ​ ∂ z 2 u ⁡ ( z , t ) + u ⁡ ( z , t ) ​ W ˙ ​ ( z , t ) \partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right) for z , t > 0 z,t>0 equipped with a Dirichlet boundary condition at 0 0 , with W ˙ \dot{W} being a Gaussian space-time noise. This equation can be seen as a degenerate analog of the parabolic Anderson model. We combine the Wiener chaos theory from Malliavin calculus, the Duhamel perturbation technique from PDEs, and the kernel analysis of (deterministic) degenerate diffusion equations to develop a solution theory for the stochastic Kimura equation. We establish results on existence, uniqueness, moments, and continuity for the solution u ⁡ ( z , t ) u\left(z,t\right) . In particular, we investigate how the stochastic potential and the degeneracy in the diffusion operator jointly affect the properties of u ⁡ ( z , t ) u\left(z,t\right) near the boundary. We also derive explicit estimates on the comparison under the L 2 − L^{2}- norm between u ⁡ ( z , t ) u\left(z,t\right) and its deterministic counterpart for ( z , t ) \left(z,t\right) within a proper range.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
60H15, 60H30, 35K65

1.
Introduction

In this work we study a class of one-dimensional stochastic degenerate diffusion equations, which we call
stochastic Kimura equations
, that are diffusion equations on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
with the diffusion coefficient degenerating linearly at the boundary
0
and subject to a Gaussian noise potential on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
This work is a marriage between the study of degenerate diffusion, which is a classical topic with a long-celebrated history, and the techniques from stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs), which is a fast developing field with many recent breakthroughs.
By combining analytic methods and stochastic analysis, we derive both qualitative (e.g., existence and uniqueness) and quantitative (e.g., moments and regularity) results on the solution to the stochastic Kimura equation.
We put a particular emphasis on the short-term behavior of the solution near the boundary, dissecting the joint effect of the degenerate diffusion operator and the space-time noise potential.
To the best of our knowledge, these aspects of stochastic degenerate diffusion equations have not been previously studied.

1.1.
Kimura Diffusion and Parabolic Anderson Model

In this subsection we give a coarse review of the two fields, degenerate diffusion equation and SPDEs, at the intersection of which our work lies.
Both fields have rich and vast literature.
Our reviews will be limited as we will only mention the results that are most relevant to the problem concerned in this work.
In particular, the two pillars based on which our work is carried out are the
Kimura diffusion
, a classical example of degenerate diffusion, and the
parabolic Anderson model
, a well studied SPDE model.
We now give a brief introduction to both models, but leave the technical details for the next section.

1.1.1. Kimura Diffusion

The Kimura diffusion is an important mathematical tool in population genetics as a model of the propagation of a certain genotype among a population
[
15
]
.
In the classical setting, it is described by the stochastic differential equation (SDE)
\mathrm{d}Z_{t}=\sqrt{2Z_{t}}\mathrm{d}B_{t}+b\left(Z_{t}\right)\mathrm{d}t
with
Z_{0}=z>0
,
b
being a drift coefficient and
0
being an absorbing boundary.
Correspondingly, the
Kimura equation
is the partial differential equation (PDE)

(1.1)

\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+b\left(z\right)\partial_{z}u\left(z,t\right),

for
z,t>0
, equipped with the Dirichlet boundary condition at
0
.
Characterized by the degeneracy in diffusion, this pair of equations has been of great interest to analysts and probabilists.
Properties of the solutions (e.g., existence, uniqueness, regularity, estimation,
etc.) have been extensively studied for the Kimura diffusion, as well as certain of its generalizations, in various contexts
[
23
,
24
,
12
,
21
,
1
]
.
Both analytic and probabilistic tool-kits have been developed
to treat degenerate diffusion operators, replacing classical techniques
that are restricted to strict elliptic/parabolic operators.

The specific Kimura diffusion that will be examined later under a stochastic potential is the model with
b\equiv 0
, but the methods developed in this study are extendable to cases with general
b
.
Let
q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)
denote the fundamental solution to
(
1.1
) with
b\equiv 0
, explicitly determined in terms of Bessel function
[
22
,
13
,
14
]
:

(1.2)

q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right):=\sqrt{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{w}}{z/w}{\frac{z}{w}}{\frac{z}{w}}}\mathchoice{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}}{t}}{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}/t}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}}{t}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}}{t}}I_{1}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2\sqrt{zw}}{t}}{2\sqrt{zw}/t}{\frac{2\sqrt{zw}}{t}}{\frac{2\sqrt{zw}}{t}}\right)

for every
z,w,t>0
.
That is, if the Kimura equation is equipped with the initial data
u_{0}\left(z\right)
, then the solution is given by

(1.3)

u_{0}\left(z,t\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}u_{0}\left(w\right)q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)dw,

for every
z,t>0
.
We will refer to
1.3
as the
deterministic solution
, which, as we will see later, serves as the starting point in the construction of the solution to the stochastic Kimura equation.

In latter sections, a perturbation of the Kimura equation will also
be considered:

\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)V\left(z,t\right),

where
V
is a potential function whose fundamental solution will be denoted by
q_{0}^{V}\left(z,w,t\right)
. When
V
is bounded and time-independent, a sharp regularity theory on
q_{0}^{V}\left(z,w,t\right)
is developed in
[
14
]
via the Duhamel perturbation method, which produces accurate estimates on the comparison between
q_{0}^{V}\left(z,w,t\right)
and
q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)
near the boundary for small times.
Details of these results will be presented in
\mathsection\ref{ssec:bgKim}
.

1.1.2. Parabolic Anderson Model

SPDEs have become a novel and popular tool in modeling phenomena observed
in scientific experiments in random environments.
Following the seminal papers
[
16
,
33
]
, many classical deterministic PDEs have been re-visited when the domain is subject to certain random noise, and a fast-growing set of theories is being developed for solutions to such SPDEs
[
16
,
33
,
5
,
29
,
31
,
35
,
8
]
.
Most notably, the parabolic Anderson model (PAM) is the classical heat equation perturbed by a Gaussian random potential.
The one-dimensional model can be written as

(1.4)

\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right),

for
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
, where
\dot{W}\left(z,t\right)
denotes a Gaussian space-time noise on some filtered probability space
\left(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}\right)
.
Formally speaking,
\{\dot{W}\left(z,t\right)\mid z\in\mathbb{R},t>0\}
can be viewed as a centered Gaussian family with covariance

(1.5)

\mathbb{E}\left[\dot{W}\left(z_{1},t_{1}\right)\dot{W}\left(z_{2},t_{2}\right)\right]=f\left(z_{1}-z_{2}\right)\gamma\left(t_{1}-t_{2}\right),

for
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
t_{1},t_{2}>0
, where
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
and
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
are two non-negative definite, symmetric, and locally integrable kernels.
To solve (
1.4
) with (deterministic) initial data
u_{0}\left(z\right)
, we seek a process
\left\{u\left(z,t\right)\mid z\in\mathbb{R},t\geq 0\right\}\subseteq L^{2}\left(\Omega\right)
, to which we refer as a
(mild) solution
to (
1.4
), such that for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
,

u\left(z,t\right)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{t}\left(z,w\right)u_{0}\left(w\right)dw+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g_{t-\tau}\left(z,w\right)u\left(w,\tau\right)W\left(dw,d\tau\right)\text{ a.s.},

where
g_{t}
\left(z,w\right)=e^{-\left|z-w\right|^{2}/2}/\sqrt{2\pi t}
is the standard heat kernel, and the stochastic integral with “respect to
W\left(dw,d\tau\right)
” is defined as a generalized Itô integral, following the theory of Malliavin calculus.
In
\mathsection\ref{ssec:bgMalCal}
, we review all the necessary elements from Malliavin calculus, including the construction and the properties of such stochastic integrals.

Common approaches toward the study of PAM combine Malliavin calculus with classical heat kernel analysis, yielding abundant results on the solution
[
16
,
33
,
6
,
4
,
3
,
10
]
.
Alternatively, another approach is the theory of rough paths developed by Hairer
[
27
,
30
]
.
The PAM has also been studied in other settings, e.g., on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

[
26
]
, on the hypercube
[
2
]
, and on Heisenberg groups
[
7
]
, as well as subject to Lévy random noise
[
8
]
.
For degenerate parabolic SPDEs, the Cauchy problem has been studied in the semilinear and quasilinear cases
[
32
,
18
]
.

1.2.
Stochastic Kimura Equation and Main Results

Inspired by the works reviewed in the previous subsection, we decide to bring the two models, the Kimura diffusion and the PAM, together and investigate the following stochastic Kimura equation:

(1.6)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right)\quad\quad z,t>0,\\
&\hskip 9.10486ptu(z,0)=u_{0}(z)\hfill z>0,\\
&\hskip 10.81204ptu(0,t)=0\hfill t>0.\end{cases}

where
\dot{W}
is the same as in (
1.5
).
Similarly as in the PAM case, a (mild) solution to (
1.6
) is a process
\left\{u\left(z,t\right)\mid z,t\geq 0\right\}\subseteq L^{2}\left(\Omega\right)

such that for every
z,t>0
,

\displaystyle u\left(z,t\right)=\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)

\displaystyle u_{0}\left(w\right)dw

(1.7)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}\left(z,w,t-\tau\right)u\left(w,\tau\right)W\left(dw,d\tau\right)\text{ a.s.}

The purpose of studying (
1.6
) has multiple folds.
First, we aim to develop a solution theory for stochastic degenerate diffusion equations, which would yield applications in modeling diffusion processes in random mediums with absorbing boundaries, e.g., genotype propagation with random hibernation/revival.
The model of the stochastic Kimura equation is a good starting point since the deterministic equation is well studied with abundant results on the fundamental solution
q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)
.
Second, we want to extend the existing literature on the PAM to the settings of degenerate diffusions and investigate how the current methods in constructing the solution to the PAM, as well as the properties of the solution, may change in the presence of degeneracy in the diffusion.
Furthermore, we are interested in studying the interaction between the degenerate diffusion operator and the stochastic potential in order to analyze their “joint effect” on the solution; to this end, we will pay particular attention to the behavior of the solution near the boundary
0
, and compare the solution to the deterministic solution in a proper sense.

In
\mathsection\ref{sec:eu}
we prove that (
1.6
) admits a solution
\left\{u\left(z,t\right)\mid z,t\geq 0\right\}
that satisfies (
1.7
) and that this solution is unique up to modification (Theorems
3.1
and
3.2
).
We adopt a strategy that is similar to the one invoked in
[
5
,
33
]
, which is to combine the Wiener chaos expansion from the Malliavin calculus and the Duhamel method from PDEs. However, the techniques we apply are substantially different from those utilized in the PAM
case, because (1) although
q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)
is explicitly determined, unlike
g_{t}\left(z,w\right)
,
q_{0}\left(z,w,t\right)
does not have a closed-form expression, nor certain desirable features such as symmetry; and (2) since our model is restricted on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, some convenient technical elements based on Fourier analysis are not applicable in our case.
We establish our result in two steps: first we solve (
1.6
) when
\dot{W}
is the
space-time white noise, i.e., when both
f
and
\gamma
in (
1.5
) are Dirac delta
\delta_{0}
, and next extend the construction of the solution to the colored noise setting.

Taking a close look at the behaviors of
u\left(z,t\right)
, we observe that the impact of the stochastic potential is most significant near the boundary, which makes the behavior of
u\left(z,t\right)
for small
z
fundamentally different from that of the solution to the deterministic Kimura equation
u_{0}\left(z,t\right)
.
To better understand the role of
\dot{W}
near the boundary, in
\mathsection\ref{sec:ratio}
we also introduce a degenerate coefficient to
\dot{W}
and consider the following stochastic Kimura equation with a degenerate noise.
That is, for some
\beta>0

(1.8)

\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\hat{z}^{\beta}\dot{W}\left(z,t\right),

where
\hat{z}:=z\wedge 1
.
Similarly, (
1.8
) also admits a unique solution
\left\{u\left(z,t\right)\mid z,t\geq 0\right\}
.
It turns out that so long as
\beta>0
, the degeneracy in the stochastic potential will “tame”
u\left(z,t\right)
enough so that it remains comparable to
u_{0}\left(z,t\right)
under the
L^{2}-
norm for some finite time
t
and for
z
all the way to the boundary.

To be specific, we prove in Theorem
3.4
that, when
u_{0}\equiv 1
and
f
satisfies some additional assumptions, there exists
T\in(0,1)
such that

(1.9)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}z>0\\
t\in[0,T]\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathchoice{\frac{u\left(z,t\right)}{u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}}{u\left(z,t\right)/u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}{\frac{u\left(z,t\right)}{u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}}{\frac{u\left(z,t\right)}{u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}}\right)^{2}\right]<\infty.

In addition, we also obtain that at every fixed
z>0
,

(1.10)

\lim_{t\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathchoice{\frac{u\left(z,t\right)}{u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}}{u\left(z,t\right)/u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}{\frac{u\left(z,t\right)}{u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}}{\frac{u\left(z,t\right)}{u_{0}\left(z,t\right)}}-1\right)^{2}\right]=0,

i.e.,
u\left(z,t\right)
stays close to
u_{0}\left(z,t\right)
, again, in the
L^{2}-
sense, for sufficiently small
t
, which is consistent with (3.10) in
[
14
]
, a result established for the deterministic equation but with a bounded potential.

In
\mathsection\ref{sec:cont}
we examine the regularity of
u\left(z,t\right)
, particularly its continuity in
z
at the boundary.
We discover that, in order to acquire boundary continuity, we need to impose further assumptions on the rate of degeneracy of the stochastic potential.
Indeed, when
\beta>1/4
we are able to prove a Hölder continuity result on
u\left(z,t\right)
in
z
all the way to the boundary in Theorem
3.8
.
Along the way, in
\mathsection\ref{sec:moment}
we also obtain results on
L^{p}-
integrability for all
p\geq 2
, as well as higher moment estimates, for
u\left(z,t\right)
for all
z,t>0
.

The aforementioned results are only part of our study of the stochastic Kimura equation as the first step in an attempt to advance our general knowledge on stochastic degenerate diffusion equations.
There are other aspects of the work that are still ongoing and will not be addressed
here.
For example, our methods can be extended to study the following two variations of (
1.6
) without substantial difficulty: (1) with an order
\alpha
of degeneracy in the diffusion operator for
\alpha
from some proper range, i.e.,

\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z^{\alpha}\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right),

and (2) with a drift coefficient
b
satisfying some proper conditions, i.e.,

\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+b\left(z\right)\partial_{z}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right).

In addition, the framework developed in this paper also allows us to study long-term asymptotics of
u\left(z,t\right)
and potentially establish results on intermittency.

1.3.
Contents and Notations

In
\mathsection\ref{sec:bg}
we review the necessary elements from the Malliavin calculus and technical results from the study of the deterministic Kimura equation.
In
\mathsection\ref{sec:results}
, we set up the model of the stochastic Kimura equation then proceed to study various aspects of the solution and prove results on existence, uniqueness, estimates, and continuity of the solution.

\mathsection\ref{sec:conclusion}
outlines some related problems and future directions.
Useful formulas on Bessel functions are deposited in
\mathsection\ref{sec:appendix}
.

We write
\mathbb{R}_{+}
to mean the interval
(0,\infty)
.
For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we write
a\vee b:=\max\left\{a,b\right\}
and
a\wedge b:=\min\left\{a,b\right\}
.
For
z>0
, we denote
\hat{z}:=z\wedge 1
.
We denote by
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2})
the set of all smooth, compactly supported functions defined on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
.
We denote by
C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
the space of all bounded continuous functions defined on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, equipped with the uniform norm

\|f\|_{u}:=\sup_{x>0}|f(x)|.

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, we denote by
I_{\alpha}
and
J_{\alpha}
the (modified) Bessel functions of the first kind of order
\alpha
.
For
q\geq p\geq 0
,
a_{1},\ldots,a_{p},b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
x\in\mathbb{C}
, we denote by

{}_{p}F_{q}\left[\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{1},\ldots,a_{p}\\
b_{1},\ldots,b_{q}\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathrel{\Big|}x\right]

the generalized Hypergeometric function.
The definitions of these special functions are reviewed in
\mathsection\ref{sec:appendix}
.

2.
Background

2.1.
The Malliavin Calculus

In this section, we present some prerequisite material regarding the Malliavin calculus which is essential for discussing the model of the stochastic Kimura equation.
For details, we refer the reader to
[
34
,
17
,
28
,
36
]
.

Let
f
and
\gamma
be as in (
1.5
), and consider the inner product

(2.1)

\left\langle\varphi,\psi\right\rangle_{H}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}}f(x-y)\gamma(r-s)\varphi(x,r)\psi(y,s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

for
\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2})
.
We define the Hilbert space
(H,\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{H})
as the completion of
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2})
with respect to
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{H}
.
It can be shown that there exists an isonormal Gaussian process
W=\left\{W(h)\mid h\in H\right\}
, on an appropriate probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W(h)]\equiv 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W(h_{1})W(h_{2})]=\left\langle h_{1},h_{2}\right\rangle_{H}.

When viewed as a map between Hilbert spaces,
W:H\to W(H)=:\mathcal{H}\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega)
, is an isometry.
This framework is convenient because it allows us to study the noise process
W
through well-behaved deterministic functions.

As it turns out, if
\mathcal{F}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
W
, then
L^{2}(\Omega)
admits a decomposition into countably many closed images of
W
under a special class of polynomials.
Let
n\geq 1
and
H_{n}
denote the
n
th Hermite polynomial.
The
n
th Wiener chaos space

\mathcal{H}_{n}
is defined as

(2.2)

\mathcal{H}_{n}:=\overline{\text{span}\left\{H_{n}(W(h))\mid h\in H,\|h\|_{H}=1\right\}},

where the closure is taken in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Then,

(2.3)

L^{2}(\Omega)=\bigoplus_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}_{n},

i.e., for any
X\in L^{2}(\Omega)
, for each
n\geq 0
there exists a unique
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
such that

(2.4)

X=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}X_{n},

where the series converges in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
We remark that since
H_{0}\equiv 1
,
\mathcal{H}_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}
is a space of constants and it can be shown that
X_{0}=\mathbb{E}[X]
.
By
[
33
]
(see the last line of page 62), for every
n\geq 1
and
p\geq 2
,
\mathcal{H}_{n}\subseteq L^{p}(\Omega)
.
In fact, if
Y\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
, then

(2.5)

\mathbb{E}[|Y|^{p}]^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{p}}{1/p}{\frac{1}{p}}{\frac{1}{p}}}\leq(p-1)^{\mathchoice{\frac{n}{2}}{n/2}{\frac{n}{2}}{\frac{n}{2}}}\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Below, we will demonstrate representations for these projections
X_{n}
in terms of stochastic integrals in the sense of Skorohod, whose moments are related to the inner product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{H}
.

To this end, we first define a suitable notion of derivative.
Let
C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
denote the space of infinitely differentiable functions
F:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
which (along with their partial derivatives) have at most polynomial growth.
We say a random variable
X\in L^{2}(\Omega)
is
smooth
if there exists
F\in C_{p}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{N})
and
h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}\in H
such that
X=F(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{N}))
.
Moreover, we denote by
\mathcal{P}\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega)
the set of all such smooth random variables.

Consequently, for some
X=F(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{N}))\in\mathcal{P}
, the
Malliavin derivative
is an unbounded, closed linear operator
D:\mathcal{P}\to L^{2}(\Omega;H)
given by

(2.6)

DX:=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{j}F(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{N}))h_{j},

where for
z,t>0
,

D_{z,t}X=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\partial_{j}F(W(h_{1}),\ldots,W(h_{N}))h_{j}(z,t),

and without loss of generality, we may assume that
h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}
are orthonormal under
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{H}
.

As with classical Sobolev spaces, we define the inner product

(2.7)

\left\langle X,Y\right\rangle_{1,2}:=\mathbb{E}[XY]+\mathbb{E}[\left\langle DX,DY\right\rangle_{H}]

for
X,Y\in\mathcal{P}
, and let
\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
denote the closure of
\mathcal{P}
with respect to
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{1,2}
.
Note that
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathbb{D}^{1,2}\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega)
, and since
\mathcal{P}
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
,
\mathcal{P}
is dense in
\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
and hence
D
can be extended to
\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
.

Viewing
D:\mathbb{D}^{1,2}\to L^{2}(\Omega;H)
, we are ready to introduce the notion of stochastic integral relevant to (
1.6
).
Let the
divergence operator

\delta
be the adjoint operator of
D
.
Namely, the domain
\text{Dom}(\delta)
of
\delta
is the set of random variables
u\in L^{2}(\Omega;H)
for which there exists
c_{u}>0
such that,

|\mathbb{E}[\left\langle DX,u\right\rangle_{H}]|\leq c_{u}\|X\|_{1,2},

for all
X\in\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
; in this case,
\delta(u)
is the unique element of
L^{2}(\Omega)
such that

(2.8)

\mathbb{E}[\delta(u)X]=\mathbb{E}[\left\langle DX,u\right\rangle_{H}].

for all
X\in\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
.
As the adjoint of an unbounded and densely defined operator,
\delta
is in turn also closed.
Furthermore, we use the notation

(2.9)

\delta(u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}u(z,t)W(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t),

where
W(\mathrm{d}z,\mathrm{d}t)=\dot{W}(z,t)\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}t
(formally,
\dot{W}(z,t)=\partial_{t}\partial_{z}W(z,t)
).
This nomenclature is motivated by the fact that the divergence operator possesses many desirable properties of a typical integral and coincides with the Itô stochastic integral in certain cases.
For this reason, we also refer to
\delta
as the
Skorohod integral
, and if
u\in\text{Dom}(\delta)
, we say that
u
is
Skorohod integrable
.

Continuing along these lines, we define the multiple Skorohod integral to arrive at representations for the random variables
X_{n}
in (
2.4
).
For
n\geq 1
, let
H^{\otimes n}
denote the
n
th tensor product of
H
, with the corresponding inner product and norm denoted by
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{n}
and
\|\cdot\|_{n}
, respectively.
Then for
f_{n}\in H^{\otimes n}
, iteratively applying the Skorohod integral yields

(2.10)

\mathcal{I}_{n}(u):=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2n}}f_{n}(z_{1},t_{1},\ldots,z_{n},t_{n})W(\mathrm{d}z_{1},\mathrm{d}t_{1})\cdots W(\mathrm{d}z_{n},\mathrm{d}t_{n}).

Given
f_{n}\in H^{\otimes n}
and
g_{m}\in H^{\otimes m}
, we denote by
\tilde{f}_{n}
the symmetrization of
f_{n}
, given by

(2.11)

\tilde{f}_{n}(z_{1},t_{1},\ldots,z_{n},t_{n}):=\mathchoice{\frac{1}{n!}}{1/n!}{\frac{1}{n!}}{\frac{1}{n!}}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}f(z_{\sigma(1)},t_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,z_{\sigma(n)},t_{\sigma(n)}),

where
S_{n}
is the set of all permutations on
n
elements.
Then
\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n})=\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tilde{f}_{n})
and

(2.12)

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n})\mathcal{I}_{m}(g_{m})]=\begin{cases}n!\langle\tilde{f}_{n},\tilde{g}_{m}\rangle_{n}&\text{ if }n=m\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\end{cases}.

Conversely, for every
n\geq 1
,
X_{n}\in\mathcal{H}_{n}
and there exists a unique
f_{n}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}
symmetric such that
X_{n}=\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n})
.
In light of this, (
2.4
) becomes

(2.13)

X=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n}),

where
\mathcal{I}_{0}(f_{0})=\mathbb{E}[X]
.
Similarly, if
u\in L^{2}(\Omega;H)
, then for every
z,t>0
, there exists a unique
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\in H^{\otimes n}
symmetric in it first
n
pairs of variables such that

(2.14)

u(z,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)),

and
\mathcal{I}_{0}(f_{0}(z,t))=\mathbb{E}[u(z,t)]
.

We close this section by presenting the following equivalent criterion for Skorohod integrability.

Proposition 2.1

(Proposition 1.3.7 of
[
36
]
)
.

Let
u\in L^{2}(\Omega;H)
with the expansion (
2.14
).
As a function
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2(n+1)}\to\mathbb{R}
,
f_{n}\in H^{\otimes(n+1)}
.
Then
u\in\text{Dom}(\delta)
if and only if the series

(2.15)

\delta(u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}(f_{n})

converges in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

To check convergence of the series (
2.15
) in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, we often make use of the property (
2.12
) of the multiple Skorohod integral.

2.2.
The Kimura Equation

In this section, we discuss the
deterministic
Kimura equation, which is the other prerequisite for studying (
1.6
).
We will rely on the results on the deterministic Kimura equation to investigate the stochastic analog. Recall that we are interested in the Kimura equation (
1.1
) with
b\equiv 0
.
In this section, following
[
14
]
, we present an introduction to the slightly more general case of a constant drift
b\equiv\nu<1
.
That is, we explore the equation

(2.16)

\partial_{t}u(z,t)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u(z,t)+\nu\partial_{z}u(z,t),

for
z,t>0
, which has the fundamental solution

(2.17)

q_{\nu}(z,w,t):=\mathchoice{\frac{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{1-\nu/2}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}}w^{\mathchoice{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\nu-1/2}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}}}{t}}{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{1-\nu/2}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}}w^{\mathchoice{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\nu-1/2}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}}/t}{\frac{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{1-\nu/2}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}}w^{\mathchoice{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\nu-1/2}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}}}{t}}{\frac{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{1-\nu/2}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}{\frac{1-\nu}{2}}}w^{\mathchoice{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\nu-1/2}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}{\frac{\nu-1}{2}}}}{t}}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}I_{1-\nu}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2\sqrt{zw}}{t}}{2\sqrt{zw}/t}{\frac{2\sqrt{zw}}{t}}{\frac{2\sqrt{zw}}{t}}\right).

In particular, for
\nu=0
, we recover (
1.2
).
Furthermore, when
\nu=0
, we have the following estimate.

Proposition 2.2
.

For every
z,w,t>0
we have

(2.18)

q_{0}(z,w,t)\leq\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t^{2}}}{z/t^{2}}{\frac{z}{t^{2}}}{\frac{z}{t^{2}}}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}}{t}}{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}/t}{\frac{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}}{t}}{\frac{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}}{t}}}

and further if
zw\geq t^{2}
, then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(2.19)

q_{0}(z,x,t)\leq C\cdot\mathchoice{\frac{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{1/4}{\frac{1}{4}}{\frac{1}{4}}}w^{-\mathchoice{\frac{3}{4}}{3/4}{\frac{3}{4}}{\frac{3}{4}}}}{\sqrt{t}}}{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{1/4}{\frac{1}{4}}{\frac{1}{4}}}w^{-\mathchoice{\frac{3}{4}}{3/4}{\frac{3}{4}}{\frac{3}{4}}}/\sqrt{t}}{\frac{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{1/4}{\frac{1}{4}}{\frac{1}{4}}}w^{-\mathchoice{\frac{3}{4}}{3/4}{\frac{3}{4}}{\frac{3}{4}}}}{\sqrt{t}}}{\frac{z^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{4}}{1/4}{\frac{1}{4}}{\frac{1}{4}}}w^{-\mathchoice{\frac{3}{4}}{3/4}{\frac{3}{4}}{\frac{3}{4}}}}{\sqrt{t}}}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}}{t}}{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}/t}{\frac{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}}{t}}{\frac{(\sqrt{z}-\sqrt{w})^{2}}{t}}}.

In addition, the authors of
[
14
]
compare the behavior of
q_{\nu}(z,w,t)
with that of the solution to (
2.16
) subject to a bounded, deterministic potential
V
:

(2.20)

\partial_{t}u^{V}(z,t)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u^{V}(z,t)+\nu\partial_{z}u^{V}(z,t)+u^{V}(z,t)V(z).

Namely, if
q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)
denotes the fundamental solution to (
2.20
), then the authors show that

(2.21)

\sup_{z,w>0}\absolutevalue{ \frac{q_\nu^V(z,w,t)}{q_\nu(z,w,t)} - 1}\leq e^{t\|V\|_{u}}-1,

which allows them to establish a sharp regularity theory on
q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)
for small
t
.

Following the Duhamel perturbation method, it is possible to express the fundamental solution
q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)
of (
2.20
) in terms of
q_{\nu}(z,w,t)
.
In
\mathsection\ref{sec:eu}
, we apply a similar method to (
1.6
); therefore, as a primer we include the Duhamel perturbation method derivation in
[
14
]
.

For
z,w,t>0
,
q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)
satisfies the integral equation

(2.22)

q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)=q_{\nu}(z,w,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{\nu}(z,x,t-\tau)q_{\nu}^{V}(x,w,\tau)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tau.

Notice that
q_{\nu}^{V}
appears recursively in the integrand.
Formally unfolding (
2.22
) once, we obtain

\displaystyle q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)

\displaystyle=q_{\nu}(z,w,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{\nu}(z,x,t-\tau)q_{\nu}^{V}(x,w,\tau)V(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=q_{\nu}(z,w,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{\nu}(z,x,t-\tau)\biggr(q_{\nu}(x,w,\tau)

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\left.+\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{0}^{\infty}\hskip-8.53581ptq_{\nu}(x,x^{\prime},\tau-\tau^{\prime})q_{\nu}(x^{\prime},w,\tau^{\prime})V(x^{\prime})\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}\right)V(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=q_{\nu}(z,w,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{\nu}(z,x,t-\tau)q_{\nu}(x,w,\tau)V(x)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}q_{\nu}(z,x,t-\tau)q_{\nu}(x,x^{\prime},\tau-\tau^{\prime})q_{\nu}^{V}(x^{\prime},w,\tau^{\prime})V(x^{\prime})V(x)\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}\mathrm{d}\tau.

Continuing this procedure, set
q_{\nu,0}(z,w,t):=q_{\nu}(z,w,t)
and for
n\geq 1
, if we inductively define

q_{\nu,n}(z,w,t):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{\nu}(z,z_{n},t-t_{n})q_{\nu,n-1}(z_{n},w,t_{n})V(z_{n})\mathrm{d}z_{n}\mathrm{d}t_{n},

then after unfolding
n
times, we obtain

\displaystyle q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)=\sum_{k=0}^{n}q_{\nu,k}(z,w,t)+\int_{\Delta_{n+1}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n+1}}q_{\nu}^{V}(z_{0},w,t_{0})\prod_{i=1}^{n+1}q_{\nu}(z_{i},z_{i-1},t_{i}-t_{i-1})V(z_{i})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{z}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{t},

where
\Delta_{n+1}t:=\left\{(t_{0},\ldots,t_{n})\in(0,t)^{n+1}\mid 0<t_{0}<\cdots<t_{n}<t\right\}
,
\mathbf{z}=(z_{0},\ldots,z_{n})
,
\mathbf{t}=(t_{0},\ldots,t_{n})
, and we put
z_{n+1}=z
and
t_{n+1}=t
.
Iterating this process at infinitum, it can be shown that

(2.23)

q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t):=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}q_{\nu,n}(z,w,t),

converges absolutely and satisfies (
2.22
).
Using the fact that
q_{\nu}
satisfies the Kolmogorov-Chapman equations, by a simple inductive argument,

q_{\nu,n}(z,w,t)\leq\mathchoice{\frac{t^{n}\|V\|_{u}^{n}}{n!}}{t^{n}\|V\|_{u}^{n}/n!}{\frac{t^{n}\|V\|_{u}^{n}}{n!}}{\frac{t^{n}\|V\|_{u}^{n}}{n!}}q_{\nu}(z,w,t),

from which (
2.21
) directly follows.

Intuitively, when
t
is small, the potential
V
has not yet generated enough “perturbation”, so
q_{\nu}^{V}(z,w,t)
is expected to be close to
q_{\nu}(z,w,t)
.
(
2.21
) not only gives a precise interpretation of this fact, since it implies that the difference

\absolutevalue{\frac{q_\nu^V(z,w,t)}{q_\nu(z,w,t)} - 1}=O(t),

for small
t
, but the estimate is also uniform in the spatial variables
(z,w)
which is sharper and more accurate than the standard heat kernel estimate.

3.
Stochastic Kimura Equation

In this section, we combine the methods from
[
5
,
4
,
33
]
and the results from
[
14
,
13
]
to study various aspects of the stochastic Kimura equation

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right)\quad\quad z,t>0,\\
&\hskip 9.10486ptu(z,0)=u_{0}(z)\hfill z>0,\\
&\hskip 10.81204ptu(0,t)=0\hfill t>0,\end{cases}.

such as existence and uniqueness of a solution, the moment bounds, and the regularity of its sample paths.
We also consider the variation wherein a degeneracy is introduced to the noise in the equation,

(3.1)

\begin{cases}&\partial_{t}u\left(z,t\right)=z\partial_{z}^{2}u\left(z,t\right)+\hat{z}^{\beta}u\left(z,t\right)\dot{W}\left(z,t\right)\quad\quad z,t>0,\\
&\hskip 9.10486ptu(z,0)=u_{0}(z)\hfill z>0,\\
&\hskip 10.81204ptu(0,t)=0\hfill t>0,\end{cases}

and investigate how the choice of
\beta>0
affects the various properties studied.
Moreover, we compare the behaviors of the solutions of (
1.6
) and (
3.1
) to that of (
2.16
) with
\nu=0
near the boundary 0 and for small times.

3.1.
Existence and Uniqueness of a Solution

In this section, we prove that (
1.6
) admits a uniqueness mild solution, up to a modification.

Definition 3.1
.

A square-integrable random field
u=\left\{u(z,t)\mid z,t\geq 0\right\}
is a
mild solution
to the stochastic Kimura equation (
1.6
) with deterministic initial condition
u_{0}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
if

(
a
)

u
has a jointly measurable modification (again denoted by
u
) and for all
z,t\geq 0
,

(3.2)

\mathbb{E}[u^{2}(z,t)]<\infty;

(
b
)

For all
z,t>0
the process
\left\{\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(\tau)q_{0}(z,w,t-\tau)u(w,\tau)\mid w,\tau>0\right\}
is Skorohod integrable and

(3.3)

u(z,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,w,t)u_{0}(w)\mathrm{d}w+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,w,t-\tau)u(w,\tau)W(\mathrm{d}w,\mathrm{d}\tau).

Suppose
u
is a mild solution to (
1.6
), then for every
z,t>0
,
u
admits the following Wiener chaos expansion

(3.4)

u(z,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)),

for some
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\in H^{\otimes n}
.
Consequently, to compute
\mathbb{E}[u^{2}(z,t)]
we now obtain

\mathbb{E}[u^{2}(z,t)]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{n}^{2}(f_{n}(\cdot;z,t))]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}n!\|\tilde{f}_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2},

so we turn our attention to finding an expression for
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)
and computing its norm.

Recall that by virtue of being a mild solution to (
1.6
),
u
satisfies the integral equation (
3.3
).
Following the method of Duhamel, unfolding (
3.3
) recursively in the Skorohod integral and denoting
u_{0}(z,t)=\mathbb{E}[u(z,t)]
yields

\displaystyle u(z,t)=u_{0}(z,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,z_{1},t_{1}-\tau)u(z_{1},t_{1})W(\mathrm{d}z_{1},\mathrm{d}t_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=u_{0}(z,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,z_{1},t_{1}-\tau)u_{0}(z_{1},t_{1})W(\mathrm{d}z_{1},\mathrm{d}t_{1})

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,z_{1},t-t_{1})q_{0}(z_{1},z_{2},t_{1}-t_{2})u(z_{2},t_{2})W(\mathrm{d}z_{1},\mathrm{d}t_{1})W(\mathrm{d}z_{2},\mathrm{d}t_{2}),

which can be repeated indefinitely.
In other words, setting
f_{0}^{\prime}=u_{0}
then recursively defining

(3.5)

\displaystyle f_{n}^{\prime}(z_{1},t_{1},\ldots,z_{n},t_{n};z,t)=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(t_{n})

\displaystyle q_{0}(z,z_{n},t-t_{n})

\displaystyle\cdot f_{n-1}^{\prime}(z_{1},t_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1},t_{n-1};z_{n},t_{n})

for
n\geq 1
, we obtain (formally) that

u(z,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n}^{\prime}(\cdot;z,t)),

and
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)=f_{n}^{\prime}(\cdot;z,t)
.
For these functions, we will slightly abuse the notation and write
n!\|\tilde{f}_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}
as
\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}
, where by direct computation

\displaystyle\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}=\int_{\Delta_{n}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}\int_{\Delta_{n}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{n}}\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}

\displaystyle q_{0}(x_{i+1},x_{i},r_{i+1}-r_{i})q_{0}(y_{i+1},y_{i},s_{i+1}-s_{i})

\displaystyle\cdot f(x_{i}-y_{i})\gamma(r_{i}-s_{i})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{s},

where
x_{n}=y_{n}=z
and
r_{n}=s_{n}=t
.

Of course, it remains to rigorously show that (
3.4
) with the derived expression in (
3.5
) for
f_{n}
is in fact the unique mild solution to (
1.6
).
We build towards the proof by first studying the case of space-time white noise via a treatment similar to
[
5
,
33
]
.
Henceforth, without loss of generality we may assume constant initial condition.
Because
u_{0}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
, all subsequent results may be recovered by first applying
u_{0}(z)\leq\|u_{0}\|_{u}
.

3.1.1.
Space-Time White Noise

In the space-time white noise case, we are interested in (
1.6
) when the centered Gaussian noise
\{\dot{W}(z,t)\mid z,t>0\}
formally has covariance

\mathbb{E}[W(z_{1},t_{1})W(z_{2},t_{2})]=\delta_{0}(z_{1}-z_{2})\delta_{0}(t_{1}-t_{2}),

where
\delta_{0}
denotes the Dirac delta.
In other words,
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{H}
coincides with the standard
L^{2}
inner product.

Given the kernels
f_{n}
defined above, we first aim to prove that for every
z,t>0
,
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\in H^{\otimes n}
.
The proof for the base case
n=0
is straightforward, but we will need the following technical lemma on
u_{0}(z,t)
.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
z,t>0
, then the following equality holds

(3.6)

u_{0}(z,t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,w,t)\mathrm{d}w=1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}},

and we have the bounds

(3.7)

e^{-1}\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}\leq u_{0}(z,t)\leq\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}\wedge 1,

where the lower bound holds for
z<t
.

Proof.

Applying
(\ref{eqn:watsonu0Int})
with
\nu=0
and
a=1
and (
5.3
), respectively, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,w,t)\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{w}}{z/w}{\frac{z}{w}}{\frac{z}{w}}}\mathchoice{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}}{t}}{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}/t}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}}{t}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z+w}{t}}{z+w/t}{\frac{z+w}{t}}{\frac{z+w}{t}}}}{t}}I_{1}\left(2\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{zw}}{t}}{\sqrt{zw}/t}{\frac{\sqrt{zw}}{t}}{\frac{\sqrt{zw}}{t}}\right)\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=2\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}/t}{\frac{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}{\frac{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}i^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{u^{2}}{t}}{u^{2}/t}{\frac{u^{2}}{t}}{\frac{u^{2}}{t}}}J_{1}\left(2\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{z}i}{t}}{\sqrt{z}i/t}{\frac{\sqrt{z}i}{t}}{\frac{\sqrt{z}i}{t}}u\right)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=2\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}/t}{\frac{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}{\frac{\sqrt{z}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}i^{-1}\cdot\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{z}i}{2}}{\sqrt{z}i/2}{\frac{\sqrt{z}i}{2}}{\frac{\sqrt{z}i}{2}}{}_{1}F_{1}\left[\hskip-2.84526pt\begin{array}[]{cc}1\\
2\end{array}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathrel{\Big|}\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}/t}{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}}{t}}\mathchoice{\frac{(e^{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}-1)t}{z}}{(e^{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}-1)t/z}{\frac{(e^{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}-1)t}{z}}{\frac{(e^{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}-1)t}{z}}

\displaystyle=1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}.

The bounds follow by taking the first-order Taylor expansion.
∎

For
n\geq 1
, due to the recursive definition of
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)
, in order to show that
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\in H^{\otimes n}
, it suffices to verify that
\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}<\infty
which we can in turn recursively compute:

(3.8)

\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,t-\tau)\|f_{n-1}(\cdot;w,\tau)\|_{n-1}^{2}\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau.

Using (
3.7
), for
n=1
we observe

\|f_{1}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{1}^{2}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,t-\tau)u_{0}^{2}(w,\tau)\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau\leq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,\tau)\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau,

which can once again be direct computed and bounded.

Lemma 3.2
.

Define
U:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by

(3.9)

U(x):=e^{-x}I_{0}(x)-\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-2x},

then for
z,t>0
,

(3.10)

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,\tau)\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau=U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}\right),

and moreover
U(z/t)\leq 1/2
.

Proof.

Per the definition of
q_{0}
and (
5.3
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\hskip-5.69054ptq_{0}^{2}(z,w,\tau)\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathchoice{\frac{zw^{-1}}{\tau^{2}}}{zw^{-1}/\tau^{2}}{\frac{zw^{-1}}{\tau^{2}}}{\frac{zw^{-1}}{\tau^{2}}}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2(z+w)}{\tau}}{2(z+w)/\tau}{\frac{2(z+w)}{\tau}}{\frac{2(z+w)}{\tau}}}I_{1}^{2}\left(2\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}{\sqrt{zw}/\tau}{\frac{\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}{\frac{\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}\right)\mathrm{d}w=\mathchoice{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}i^{2}}}{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}/\tau^{2}i^{2}}{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}i^{2}}}{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}i^{2}}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\hskip-5.69054ptw^{-1}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2w}{\tau}}{2w/\tau}{\frac{2w}{\tau}}{\frac{2w}{\tau}}}J_{1}^{2}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2i\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}{2i\sqrt{zw}/\tau}{\frac{2i\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}{\frac{2i\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}\right)\mathrm{d}w.

Next, we apply Proposition
5.6
to obtain

i^{-2}\int_{0}^{\infty}w^{-1}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2w}{\tau}}{2w/\tau}{\frac{2w}{\tau}}{\frac{2w}{\tau}}}J_{1}^{2}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2i\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}{2i\sqrt{zw}/\tau}{\frac{2i\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}{\frac{2i\sqrt{zw}}{\tau}}\right)\mathrm{d}w=e^{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}\left(I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)-I_{1}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\right)-1,

and hence we get

(3.11)

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,\tau)\mathrm{d}w=\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}/\tau^{2}}{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{\frac{ze^{-\mathchoice{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{2z/\tau}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}{\frac{2z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}\left(e^{\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}\left(I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)-I_{1}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\right)-1\right)\mathrm{d}\tau.

Via integration by parts,

\displaystyle z\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}/\tau^{2}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}I_{1}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}\derivative{\tau}\left[I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\right]\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\Big|_{0}^{t}+z\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}/\tau^{2}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}}I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}\right)+z\int_{0}^{t}\mathchoice{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}/\tau^{2}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}{\frac{e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}}}{\tau^{2}}}I_{0}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{\tau}}{z/\tau}{\frac{z}{\tau}}{\frac{z}{\tau}}\right)\mathrm{d}\tau,

and substituting this last expression into (
3.11
), (
3.10
) directly follows.
Finally,
\|U\|_{u}=1/2
follows from straightforward calculations.
∎

Once again, we have a uniform bound for the norm.
This reveals the main pillar of our strategy - using induction, and Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
to conclude that

(3.12)

\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}\leq\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}{1/2^{n}}{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}.

Indeed, the case
n=0
is confirmed by Lemma
3.1
.
Next, assuming (
3.12
) holds for
n-1
for some
n\geq 1
, then by (
3.8
),

\displaystyle\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,t-\tau)\|f_{n-1}(\cdot;w,\tau)\|_{n}^{2}\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau,

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-n+1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z,w,t-\tau)\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau=\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}{1/2^{n}}{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}.

Hence it directly follows that
f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\in H^{\otimes n}
and we may set
u_{n}(z,t):=\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n}(\cdot;z,t))
for notational simplicity.
We are now equipped to present the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.1
.

When
W
is the space-time white noise,
u=\left\{u(z,t)\mid z,t\geq 0\right\}
as in (
3.4
) is well-defined as a convergent series in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Moreover,

(3.14)

\sup_{z,t\geq 0}\mathbb{E}[u^{2}(z,t)]\leq 2,

and
u
is the unique mild solution to (
1.6
).

Proof.

The convergence of the series in (
3.4
) to
u(z,t)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and (
3.14
) follow by the summability of the bounds (
3.12
) over
n\geq 0
.

To see that
u
has a jointly measurable modification, we first show that
(z,t)\mapsto u(z,t)
is continuous in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
To this end, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|u_{n}(z_{1},t_{1})-u_{n}(z_{2},t_{2})|^{2}]=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\absolutevalue{q_0(z_1,w,t_1-\tau) - q_0(z_2,w,t_2-\tau)}^{2}\mathbb{E}[u_{n-1}^{2}(w,\tau)]\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-n+1}\int_{0}^{t_{1}\wedge t_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\absolutevalue{q_0(z_1,w,t_1-\tau) - q_0(z_2,w,t_2-\tau)}^{2}\mathrm{d}w\mathrm{d}\tau,

where the last bound vanishes as
(z_{1},t_{1})\to(z_{2},t_{2})
by the dominated convergence theorem and continuity of the fundamental solution
q_{0}
.
By the continuity of
u_{n}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
and (
3.14
), it directly follows that
u
is continuous in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and in turn by Theorem 30 in Chapter IV of
[
19
]
,
u
possesses a jointly measurable modification.
We denote this modification again by
u
.
We have verified condition
(\textbf{a})
in Definition
3.1
.

Next, we check condition
(\textbf{b})
.
For
z,w,t-\tau,\tau>0
, let

(3.15)

u^{(z,t)}(w,\tau):=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(\tau)q_{0}(z,w,t-\tau)u(w,\tau).

By measurability of
u
, it is immediate that
u^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)
is adapted to
W
.
Moreover by (
3.14
),
\mathbb{E}[(u^{(z,t)}(w,\tau))^{2}]<\infty
and so
u^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)
admits the Wiener chaos expansion

u^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}\left(g_{n}^{(z,t)}(\cdot;w,\tau)\right),

where
g_{n}^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(\tau)q_{0}(z,w,t-\tau)f_{n}(\cdot;w,\tau)=f_{n+1}(\cdot,w,\tau;z,t)
.
Clearly,
g_{n}^{(z,t)}\in H^{\otimes(n+1)}
by the corresponding properties for
u
and
f_{n+1}
, respectively, and

\delta\left(u^{(z,t)}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\left(g_{n}^{(z,t)}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\left(f_{n+1}(\cdot;z,t)\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}u_{n}(z,t)

converges in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Therefore
u^{(z,t)}
is indeed Skorohod integrable and by Proposition
2.1
(1.3.7 of
[
36
]
),

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z,w,t-\tau)u(w,\tau)W(\mathrm{d}w,\mathrm{d}\tau)

\displaystyle=\delta\left(u^{(z,t)}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\left(f_{n+1}(\cdot;z,t)\right)=u(z,t)-u_{0}(z,t).

Thus,
u
also satisfies condition
(\textbf{b})
and is in fact a mild solution of (
1.6
).

Next, we prove that
u
is the unique mild solution to (
1.6
).
Let
v=\left\{v(z,t):z,t\geq 0\right\}
be another solution to (
1.6
) with Wiener chaos expansion

v(z,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(k_{n}(\cdot;z,t))

for some symmetric non-negative functions
k_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\in H^{\otimes n}
and
z,t>0
fixed.
We will show that
k_{n}(\cdot;z,t)=\tilde{f}_{n}(\cdot,z,t)
for every
n\geq 1
.

Let
v^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)
be defined similarly to (
3.15
).
Then by definition of a solution, the process
v^{(z,t)}:=\left\{v^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)|w,\tau>0\right\}
is Skorohod integrable and admits the Wiener chaos expansion

v^{(z,t)}(w,\tau)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}\left(h_{n}^{(z,t)}(\cdot;w,\tau)\right),

where
h_{n}^{(z,t)}(\cdot;w,\tau)=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(\tau)q_{0}(z,w,t-\tau)k_{n}(\cdot;w,\tau)
.
By Proposition
2.1
,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\left(k_{n+1}(\cdot;z,t)\right)=v(z,t)-\mathbb{E}[v(z,t)]=\delta\left(v^{(z,t)}\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n+1}\left(\widetilde{h_{n}^{(z,t)}}\right),

from which it follows that
k_{n+1}(\cdot,z,t)=\widetilde{h_{n}^{(z,t)}}
by the uniqueness of the Wiener chaos expansion with symmetric kernels.

We now express
k_{n}(\cdot,z,t)
recursively.
For
n=0
, we obtain

k_{1}(z_{1},t_{1},z,t)=\widetilde{h_{0}^{(z,t)}}(z_{1},t_{1})=h_{0}^{(z,t)}(z_{1},t_{1})=\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(t_{1})q_{0}(z,z_{1},t-t_{1})\left(1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}}\right).

For
n=1
, we obtain

\displaystyle k_{2}(z_{1},t_{1},z_{2},t_{2},z,t)=\widetilde{h_{1}^{(z,t)}}(z_{1},t_{1},z_{2},t_{2})=\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\left[h_{1}^{(z,t)}(z_{1},t_{1},z_{2},t_{2})+h_{1}^{(z,t)}(z_{2},t_{2},z_{1},t_{1})\right]=

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(t_{2})q_{0}(z,z_{2},t-t_{2})k_{1}(z_{1},t_{1},z_{2},t_{2})+\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(t_{1})q_{0}(z,z_{1},t-t_{1})k_{1}(z_{2},t_{2},z_{1},t_{1})\right].

Using the expression for
k_{1}
, we conclude

\displaystyle k_{2}(z_{1},t_{1},z_{2},t_{2},z,t)=

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(t_{2})q_{0}(z,z_{2},t-t_{2})\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t_{2})}(t_{1})q_{0}(z_{2},z_{1},t_{2}-t_{1})\left(1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t)}(t_{1})q_{0}(z,z_{1},t-t_{1})\mathbbm{1}_{(0,t_{1})}(t_{2})q_{0}(z_{1},z_{2},t_{1}-t_{2})\left(1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{z_{2}/t_{2}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}}\right)\right],

that is,
k_{2}(\cdot,z,t)=\tilde{f}_{2}(\cdot,z,t)
.
Iterating this procedure, we infer that
k_{n}(\cdot,z,t)=\tilde{f}_{n}(\cdot,z,t)
for every
n\geq 1
.
Hence,

v(z,t)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(k_{n}(\cdot,z,t))=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tilde{f}_{n}(\cdot,z,t))=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{n}(f_{n}(\cdot,z,t))=u(z,t).

That is, the solution
u
given by (
3.4
) is the unique mild solution to (
1.6
), up to a modification.
∎

3.1.2.
Colored Noise

We continue to the case of colored noise.
That is,
W=\{\dot{W}(z,t)\mid z,t>0\}
is a centered Gaussian process whose covariance is formally given by

\mathbb{E}\left[\dot{W}(z_{1},t_{1})\dot{W}(z_{2},t_{2})\right]=f(z_{1}-z_{2})\gamma(t_{1}-t_{2}),

for
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2}>0
, where
f,\gamma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
are non-negative definite, symmetric, and locally integrable kernels.

As in the previous section, we seek to show that the process
u
defined in (
3.4
) is the unique mild solution to (
1.6
) when
W
is a colored noise.
To this end, we show that the series in (
3.4
) converges in
L^{2}(\Omega)
for all
z,t>0
and we recursively compute
\left\langle f_{n}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{n}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{n}
:

(3.16)

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{n}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{n}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{n}=\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-39.83385pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\hskip-8.53581ptf(x-y)

\displaystyle\cdot q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\left\langle f_{n-1}(\cdot;x,r),f_{n-1}(\cdot;y,s)\right\rangle_{n-1}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

for
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2}>0
and
n\geq 1
.

However, here the main challenge arises, unlike in (
3.13
), after we apply Lemma
3.1
for
n=1
, we arrive at

\left\langle f_{1}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{1}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{1}\leq\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

which cannot be directly computed as in Lemma
3.2
.
Instead, we seek to extract
\gamma
and
f
from the integrals by imposing the following additional conditions:

(
i
)

f
is non-increasing on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

(
ii
)

for all
\varepsilon>0
,
\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}f(x)\mathrm{d}x<\infty
and

\lim_{\varepsilon\searrow 0}\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}f(x)\mathrm{d}x=0.

In addition, for
\varepsilon,t>0
we introduce the following notation

(3.17)

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon}:=\left\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\mid|x-y|<\varepsilon\right\},

(3.18)

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}:=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}f(x)\mathrm{d}x=2\int_{0}^{\varepsilon}f(x)\mathrm{d}x,

(3.19)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t}:=\int_{-t}^{t}\gamma(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=2\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau,.

Note these quantities are well-defined by local-integrability and symmetry of
f
and
\gamma
.
We proceed to make our strategy concrete.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2}>0
and let
U
be as in (
3.9
).
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
we have

(3.20)

\left\langle f_{1}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{1}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{1}\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}\right).

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and define

(3.21)

\displaystyle L_{1,0}

\displaystyle:=\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

(3.22)

\displaystyle L_{2,0}

\displaystyle:=\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}^{c}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

Then by (
3.7
), we’ll treat the two terms (
3.21
) and (
3.22
) individually.
By Proposition
5.2
applied twice (once for the spatial variables, once for the temporal variables), we get

\displaystyle L_{1,0}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)F_{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}y\right)^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}\left(\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}r\int_{0}^{t_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}^{2}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Notice that these last integrals are precisely quantities studied in the white noise case.
Therefore, Lemma
3.2
immediately implies that

(3.23)

L_{1,0}\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}\right)U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{z_{2}/t_{2}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}\right)}.

On the other hand, by our assumptions on
f
, we have

\displaystyle L_{2,0}

\displaystyle\leq f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)\mathrm{d}x\int_{0}^{\infty}q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)u_{0}(z_{1},t_{1}-r)u_{0}(z_{2},t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

Again, the above integrals are also quantities studied in the white noise case.
This time by Lemma
3.1
, it follows that

L_{2,0}\leq f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\left(1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}-r}}{z_{1}/t_{1}-r}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}-r}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}-r}}}\right)\left(1-e^{-\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}-s}}{z_{2}/t_{2}-s}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}-s}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}-s}}}\right)\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s\leq f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

By applying Proposition
5.2
once again,

(3.24)

L_{2,0}\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}.

Substituting the bounds (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), we achieve the stated result (
3.20
).
∎

As in the space-time white noise case, we obtain a bound uniform in
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.
Moreover, the bound in (
3.20
) is increasing with respect to the time variables.
Hence, it is tempting to replicate (
3.12
) and obtain a geometric bound

\displaystyle\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,t]^{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\left\langle f_{n-1}(\cdot;x,r),f_{n-1}(\cdot;y,s)\right\rangle_{n-1}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,t]^{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\left(\Gamma_{r\vee s}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}\right)\right)^{n-1}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t}^{n-1}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)t\right)^{n-1}\int_{[0,t]^{2}}\hskip-17.07164pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

Thus, it follows from here that

(3.25)

\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}\leq\Gamma_{t}^{n}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)t\right)^{n},

for the
L^{2}
-norm, but the following remark demonstrates that such an argument may potentially fail to hold for general
f
.

Remark 3.1
.

Suppose
\gamma(z)=f(z)=|z|^{-1/2}
, then

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\|f_{n}(\cdot;z,t)\|_{n}^{2}\leq\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}4^{n}t^{\mathchoice{\frac{n}{2}}{n/2}{\frac{n}{2}}{\frac{n}{2}}}\left(2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+\mathchoice{\frac{t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{t/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\frac{t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}\right),

and whose right hand side converges if and only if

4^{n}t^{\mathchoice{\frac{n}{2}}{n/2}{\frac{n}{2}}{\frac{n}{2}}}\left(2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+\mathchoice{\frac{t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{t/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\frac{t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{t}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}\right)<1.

Yet for
t
sufficiently large, no choice of
\varepsilon
exists for which this condition holds.

In light of Remark
3.1
, we are forced to derive tighter bounds.
As an example, let us examine the case
n=2
.
As before, we arrive at

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{2}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{2}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-39.83385pt\gamma(r-s)\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}\right)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}\hskip-9.95845ptf(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

Define

(3.26)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptL_{1,0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-39.83385pt\gamma(r-s)\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}\right)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}\hskip-9.95845ptf(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptL_{2,0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-39.83385pt\gamma(r-s)\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}\right)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}^{c}}\hskip-9.95845ptf(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

Then by similar arguments to those in the proof of Lemma
3.3
,
we obtain

\displaystyle L_{1,0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}+f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}\right)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z,x,t-r)q_{0}(z,y,t-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}^{2}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}/4}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}+\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}\right),

and

L_{2,0}^{\prime}\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}^{2}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}+f(\varepsilon)^{2}\mathchoice{\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{2}}{t_{1}t_{2}/2}{\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{2}}{\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{2}}\right).

Putting it all together, we have

(3.28)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\left\langle f_{2}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{2}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{2}\leq L_{1,0}^{\prime}+L_{2,0}^{\prime}\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}^{2}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}/4}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}+F_{\varepsilon}f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}+f(\varepsilon)^{2}\mathchoice{\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{2}}{t_{1}t_{2}/2}{\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{2}}{\frac{t_{1}t_{2}}{2}}\right).

Again, we obtain a bound uniform in
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
and increasing in
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
, with powers of
\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}},F(\varepsilon),
and
f(\varepsilon)
appearing throughout.
The key insight for generalizing to higher order Wiener chaos spaces is that this approach, which we have already applied twice, can be reiterated indefinitely to obtain a bound which maintains its general pattern.
As it turns out, it can be shown that this pattern possesses a tree-like structure (see Figure
3.1
).

n=0
n=1
n=2
1
\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}
\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t}^{2}F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}{\Gamma_{t}^{2}F_{\varepsilon}^{2}/4}{\frac{\Gamma_{t}^{2}F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t}^{2}F_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{4}}
\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t}^{n}F_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{2^{n}}}{\Gamma_{t}^{n}F_{\varepsilon}^{n}/2^{n}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t}^{n}F_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{2^{n}}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t}^{n}F_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{2^{n}}}
\Gamma_{t}^{2}\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}f(\varepsilon)t
\Gamma_{t}\displaystyle f(\varepsilon)t
\Gamma_{t}^{2}\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}f(\varepsilon)t
\Gamma_{t}^{2}\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{2}}{2}}{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{2}/2}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{2}}{2}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{2}}{2}}
\Gamma_{t}^{n}\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{n}}{n!}}{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{n}/n!}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{n}}{n!}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{n}}{n!}}
\Gamma_{t}^{n}\displaystyle\binom{m-1}{k}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{n-k-1}\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{k+1}/(k+1)!}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)t)^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}
+
+
+
+
+\cdots+
+\cdots+

Figure 3.1.
The left child of each vertex corresponds to the bound obtained by handling the integral over
A_{\varepsilon}
while the right child corresponds to the integral over
A_{\varepsilon}^{c}
.
Each
1\leq m\leq n
corresponds to the
m
th subtree from the left whose respective roots are the right children of the leftmost vertex at each depth.
Moreover, for any given path in a subtree, each
0\leq k\leq m-1
corresponds to the number of rights in the path.

Therefore, in order to rigorously prove an analog to (
3.12
), we need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2}>0
and let
k\geq 0
.
Then for
U
as in (
3.9
) and any
\varepsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)(rs)^{\mathchoice{\frac{k}{2}}{k/2}{\frac{k}{2}}{\frac{k}{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

(3.29)

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}\left(F_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}\right)U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{z_{2}/t_{2}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}\right)t_{1}^{k}t_{2}^{k}}+\mathchoice{\frac{f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}}{k+1}}{f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}/k+1}{\frac{f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}}{k+1}}{\frac{f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}}{k+1}}\right).

Proof.

Following a similar strategy to Lemma
3.3
, define
A_{\varepsilon}
as before and

(3.30)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptL_{1,k}

\displaystyle:=\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)(rs)^{\mathchoice{\frac{k}{2}}{k/2}{\frac{k}{2}}{\frac{k}{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptL_{2,k}

\displaystyle:=\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}^{c}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)(rs)^{\mathchoice{\frac{k}{2}}{k/2}{\frac{k}{2}}{\frac{k}{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

We easily treat (
3.30
) by reusing the bound for
L_{1,0}
in (
3.21
):

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274ptL_{1,k}

\displaystyle\leq(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k}{2}}{k/2}{\frac{k}{2}}{\frac{k}{2}}}\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{A_{\varepsilon}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

(3.32)

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}\sqrt{U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}\right)U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{z_{2}/t_{2}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}\right)t_{1}^{k}t_{2}^{k}}.

To bound (
3.31
), we again apply a similar strategy used to obtain the bound for
L_{2,0}
in (
3.22
):

\displaystyle L_{2,k}

\displaystyle\leq f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)(rs)^{\mathchoice{\frac{k}{2}}{k/2}{\frac{k}{2}}{\frac{k}{2}}}u_{0}(z_{1},t_{1}-r)u_{0}(z_{2},t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq f(\varepsilon)\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)(rs)^{\mathchoice{\frac{k}{2}}{k/2}{\frac{k}{2}}{\frac{k}{2}}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}f(\varepsilon)\left(\int_{0}^{t_{1}}r^{k}\mathrm{d}r\int_{0}^{t_{2}}s^{k}\mathrm{d}s\right)^{\mathchoice{\frac{1}{2}}{1/2}{\frac{1}{2}}{\frac{1}{2}}}

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}}{k+1}}{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}/k+1}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}}{k+1}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}f(\varepsilon)(t_{1}t_{2})^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}}{k+1}},

where the penultimate inequality follows per usual by Proposition
5.2
.
Hence, the result of the lemma following by summing (
3.32
) and (
3.33
).
∎

We are now equipped to present the solution to (
1.6
).

Theorem 3.2
.

Suppose
f
satisfies conditions (
i
) and (
ii
).
Then for every
z,t>0
,
u(z,t)
as in (
3.4
) is well-defined as a convergent series in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Moreover for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (
\varepsilon
depends on
t)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[u^{2}(z,t)]\leq u_{0}(z,t)^{2}+

\displaystyle U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z}{t}}{z/t}{\frac{z}{t}}{\frac{z}{t}}\right)\mathchoice{\frac{2\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{2-\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}{2\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}/2-\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{2-\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}{2-\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}

(3.34)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathchoice{\frac{\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{t}f(\varepsilon)t}{\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}}{\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{t}f(\varepsilon)t/\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{t}f(\varepsilon)t}{\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}}{\frac{\sqrt{2}\Gamma_{t}f(\varepsilon)t}{\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}}\exp\left\{\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)t\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}{F_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}})}}{2f(\varepsilon)t\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}/F_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}})}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)t\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}{F_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}})}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)t\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}}}{F_{\varepsilon}(\sqrt{2}-\sqrt{\Gamma_{t}F_{\varepsilon}})}}\right\}.

u=\left\{u(z,t)\mid z,t\geq 0\right\}
is the unique mild solution to (
1.6
).

Proof.

We focus on the convergence of the series in (
3.4
) to
u(z,t)
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.
The rest of the theorem follows similarly as in the proof of Theorem
3.1
for the case of space-time white noise.

Let
z_{1},z_{2},t_{1},t_{2},\varepsilon>0
, we begin by inductively showing

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{n}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{n}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{n}\leq\left(\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{n}\left[2\sqrt{U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}\right)U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{z_{2}/t_{2}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}\right)}\right.

(3.35)

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt+\left.\sum_{m=1}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\mathchoice{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{1/(k+1)!}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}/F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}\right)^{k+1}\right]

for
n\geq 1
.
The base case
n=1
is proved in Lemma
3.3
.
Next, let
n\geq 2
and suppose (
3.35
) holds for
n-1
.
Then by (
3.16
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle f_{n}(\cdot;z_{1},t_{1}),f_{n}(\cdot;z_{2},t_{2})\right\rangle_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\left(\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{r\vee s}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\Gamma_{r\vee s}F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{\Gamma_{r\vee s}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{\Gamma_{r\vee s}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{n-1}\left[1+\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\mathchoice{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{1/(k+1)!}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}/F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{rs}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}\right)^{k+1}\right]\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\left(\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{n-1}\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\left(\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{n-1}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\mathchoice{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{1/(k+1)!}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{2f(\varepsilon)/F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)}{F_{\varepsilon}}}\right)^{k+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\cdot\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[0,t_{2}]}\hskip-34.1433pt\gamma(r-s)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}}f(x-y)q_{0}(z_{1},x,t_{1}-r)q_{0}(z_{2},y,t_{2}-s)(rs)^{\mathchoice{\frac{k+1}{2}}{k+1/2}{\frac{k+1}{2}}{\frac{k+1}{2}}}\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}s.

By applying Lemma
3.4
to both integrals, it then follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[u_{n}(z_{1},t_{1})u_{n}(z_{2},t_{2})\right]\leq\left(\mathchoice{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{\Gamma_{t_{1}\vee t_{2}}F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{n}\left[2\sqrt{U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{z_{1}/t_{1}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}{\frac{z_{1}}{t_{1}}}\right)U\left(\mathchoice{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{z_{2}/t_{2}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}{\frac{z_{2}}{t_{2}}}\right)}+\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}/F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\left(\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{-k-1}\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}/(k+1)!}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}+\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{-k-2}\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}}{(k+2)!}}{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}/(k+2)!}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}}{(k+2)!}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}}{(k+2)!}}\right)\right].

Regrouping terms, we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}

\displaystyle\left(\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{-k-1}\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}/(k+1)!}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+1}}{(k+1)!}}+\left(\mathchoice{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{F_{\varepsilon}/2}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}{\frac{F_{\varepsilon}}{2}}\right)^{-k-2}\mathchoice{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}}{(k+2)!}}{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}/(k+2)!}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}}{(k+2)!}}{\frac{(f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}})^{k+2}}{(k+2)!}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=2}^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\binom{m-1}{k}\mathchoice{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{1/(k+1)!}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}{\frac{1}{(k+1)!}}\left(\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}/F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}\right)^{k+1},

and

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}/F_{\varepsilon}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}{\frac{2f(\varepsilon)\sqrt{t_{1}t_{2}}}{F_{\varepsilon}}}
